This evaluation procedure is developed and then demonstrated using multiple image data sets, both real and simulated. The main result is that when the subjectivity and intrinsic deficiencies of any feature-encoding choice are put upfront, Tversky's method leads to intuitive results, whereas traditional methods based on summarizing distances in deep feature spaces do not.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the provided paper text:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of evaluating machine-generated biomedical images, where ground truth is often unavailable. It proposes using the Tversky Index, a set-theoretic similarity measure, as an evaluation metric. The method involves binarizing feature values from images and then calculating a weighted similarity index based on the overlap of these features. The authors demonstrate the approach using simulated and real medical image datasets, showing that the Tversky Index can provide intuitive results, especially when compared to traditional distance-based metrics like Kullback-Leibler divergence on deep feature embeddings. They argue that the Tversky Index, because it focuses on tallying features, is more interpretable and less prone to ambiguity than distance-based methods. Stochastic image models are used to add features and test the ability of each method. Real patient data are used to test similarity under different conditions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in applying the Tversky Index, in this specific way (with binarization and feature weighting), to the evaluation of *machine-generated biomedical images*. While the Tversky Index is a well-established measure, its application to this specific problem, with an emphasis on feature specification and comparison against deep feature methods, is a contribution. It explicitly reframes the evaluation problem from a distance calculation to a feature tally. Other groups have previously applied the Tversky index to loss functions in segmentation, so this paper isn't the first time that the Tversky index has been used in a machine learning setting. However, this paper reframes the problem as one of similarity by labels rather than distances, and attempts to measure the labels themselves.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant problem in medical imaging: the robust evaluation of synthetic data. Machine-generated images are increasingly used for data augmentation, super-resolution, and other tasks, but their quality assessment is crucial. The use of the Tversky Index offers a potentially more reliable and interpretable evaluation method than relying solely on deep feature distances, which can be difficult to interpret clinically. A major benefit from a clinical point of view is that it focuses on the presences of the labels themselves rather than a calculation of distance. Given the increasing use of synthetic medical imaging, a robust set of similarity assessments is highly desired.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges of evaluating machine-generated images.
    *   **Well-Defined Methodology:** The proposed method, including feature binarization and weighted Tversky Index calculation, is well-defined.
    *   **Demonstration on Multiple Datasets:** The approach is demonstrated on both simulated and real medical image datasets, increasing its credibility.
    *   **Comparison with Existing Methods:** The comparison with Kullback-Leibler divergence and other distance based measures is important to show the strength of this new method.
    *   **Clear Interpretation:** The paper provides clear interpretations of the results, highlighting the advantages of the Tversky Index in terms of interpretability and intuitiveness.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Subjectivity of Feature Selection:** The choice of features remains subjective, which can influence the evaluation results. The paper acknowledges this, but a more robust method for feature selection would strengthen the approach. The paper suggests, in the "Future Work" section, that it intends to rigorously derive the features used in the method.
    *   **Reliance on Binary Features:** Binarizing features may lose information compared to using continuous feature values directly.
    *   **Limited Generalizability:** While the paper demonstrates the approach on several datasets, the results may not generalize to all types of medical images and tasks. The conclusion section speaks to generalizability by showing that the method is generalizable by the discovery of manifolds of "relevant features."
    *   **Limited Analysis:** The analysis of performance on real datasets is not in-depth. Error cases and failure modes could be added.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the evaluation of machine-generated medical images by offering a more interpretable and clinically relevant alternative to traditional distance-based metrics. It is more explainable for clinical application.

**Score:** 6.5

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel approach to an important problem, with a clear methodology and promising initial results. However, it also has some limitations, particularly in the subjectivity of feature selection and the generalizability of the findings. The approach offers some advantages over existing deep feature techniques, which may make it useful. The biggest strength is its ease of interpretation, which will likely be a highly desired outcome for clinicians using machine generated images.

Score: 6.5

- **Classification**: eess.IV
- **Score**: 6/10

### QuestBench: Can LLMs ask the right question to acquire information in reasoning tasks?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.22674v1)
- **Authors**: Belinda Z. Li, Been Kim, Zi Wang
- **Abstract**: Recently, a large amount of work has focused on improving large language models' (LLMs') performance on reasoning benchmarks such as math and logic. However, past work has largely assumed that tasks are well-defined. In the real world, queries to LLMs are often underspecified, only solvable through acquiring missing information. We formalize this as a constraint satisfaction problem (CSP) with missing variable assignments. Using a special case of this formalism where only one necessary variable assignment is missing, we can rigorously evaluate an LLM's ability to identify the minimal necessary question to ask and quantify axes of difficulty levels for each problem. We present QuestBench, a set of underspecified reasoning tasks solvable by asking at most one question, which includes: (1) Logic-Q: Logical reasoning tasks with one missing proposition, (2) Planning-Q: PDDL planning problems with initial states that are partially-observed, (3) GSM-Q: Human-annotated grade school math problems with one missing variable assignment, and (4) GSME-Q: a version of GSM-Q where word problems are translated into equations by human annotators. The LLM is tasked with selecting the correct clarification question(s) from a list of options. While state-of-the-art models excel at GSM-Q and GSME-Q, their accuracy is only 40-50% on Logic-Q and Planning-Q. Analysis demonstrates that the ability to solve well-specified reasoning problems may not be sufficient for success on our benchmark: models have difficulty identifying the right question to ask, even when they can solve the fully specified version of the problem. Furthermore, in the Planning-Q domain, LLMs tend not to hedge, even when explicitly presented with the option to predict ``not sure.'' This highlights the need for deeper investigation into models' information acquisition capabilities.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "QuestBench: Can LLMs ask the right question to acquire information in reasoning tasks?":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces QUESTBENCH, a new benchmark designed to evaluate the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to proactively acquire missing information needed to solve reasoning tasks. The benchmark focuses on scenarios where tasks are underspecified, formalized as Constraint Satisfaction Problems (CSPs) with missing variable assignments.  QUESTBENCH includes four distinct tasks: Logic-Q (logical reasoning with a missing proposition), Planning-Q (PDDL planning problems with partially observed initial states), GSM-Q (grade school math problems with a missing variable), and GSME-Q (equation-based GSM-Q). The paper evaluates several state-of-the-art LLMs on QUESTBENCH, finding that while they perform well on GSM-Q and GSME-Q, their accuracy significantly drops on Logic-Q and Planning-Q. The authors analyze the difficulty axes of each task, revealing that models struggle to identify the right question, even when capable of solving the fully specified problem. They also find that LLMs tend not to hedge in Planning-Q even when given the option to predict "not sure," highlighting a need for better information acquisition capabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in formalizing the information acquisition problem as underspecified CSPs and creating a benchmark specifically designed to evaluate LLMs' ability to ask the right clarifying questions in reasoning contexts. Existing benchmarks often assume well-defined tasks, so this paper addresses a critical gap in evaluating LLMs' real-world problem-solving abilities.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its focus on a crucial, often overlooked aspect of LLM reasoning: the capacity to identify and address missing information.  The benchmark provides a rigorous way to assess this capability, revealing limitations in current LLMs' ability to do so, particularly in more complex domains like logic and planning. This has implications for deploying LLMs in real-world settings where tasks are rarely perfectly defined. The analysis of problem difficulty axes (search depth, number of constraints, etc.) provides valuable insights into what factors influence LLM performance on these types of tasks.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Formalization:**  The formalization of the underspecified reasoning task as a CSP is well-defined and allows for a structured analysis.

    *   **Comprehensive Benchmark:** QUESTBENCH offers a diverse set of tasks across different reasoning domains, facilitating a broader evaluation of LLMs' abilities.

    *   **Rigorous Evaluation:** The multi-choice question-asking format enables a clear ground truth for accuracy evaluation, unlike subjective or ambiguous tasks. The authors present extensive experimental results across various LLMs and prompting methods.

    *   **Insightful Analysis:** The analysis of problem difficulty axes, ablation studies, and correlations provide valuable insights into the strengths and weaknesses of LLMs in information acquisition.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Task Simplification:**  While the multi-choice format provides a clear evaluation metric, it oversimplifies real-world question-asking, where models need to generate questions rather than select from a predefined set.

    *   **Limited Complexity of Logic-Q and Planning-Q:** The search depth and variable/constraint numbers are still somewhat limited, potentially not fully capturing the complexities of true real-world logic and planning problems. The blocksworld domain is a bit toyish despite being common.

    *   **Emphasis on Math:** The relative strength in GSM/GSME-Q compared to Logic/Planning-Q could be partly due to LLMs being extensively pre-trained on math datasets and benchmarks, leading to an overestimation of their overall reasoning ability.

    *   **Lack of "Open-Ended" Question Generation:** The benchmark primarily assesses the ability to *select* the correct question from a list. It doesn't evaluate the ability of LLMs to *generate* relevant and coherent questions from scratch.
    *   **Domain specificity of some design choices** some modeling and analysis choices are very closely tied to the specifics of each domain and this might limit the ability to draw more general conclusions

*   **Potential Influence:**  QUESTBENCH has the potential to significantly influence research on LLMs by highlighting the importance of information acquisition and providing a valuable tool for evaluating progress in this area. It could encourage researchers to develop new techniques for improving LLMs' ability to proactively gather missing information and handle underspecified tasks. It also provides a template for other datasets that address real-world reasoning tasks.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a novel and well-executed benchmark that addresses a significant gap in evaluating LLMs' reasoning capabilities.  The rigorous experimental evaluation and insightful analysis contribute to a deeper understanding of current LLMs' limitations in information acquisition. While the task simplification and limited complexity of some tasks represent minor weaknesses, the strengths of the paper outweigh these limitations. QUESTBENCH is a valuable contribution that has the potential to shape future research in this area and influence the development of more robust and reliable LLMs.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### DSO: Aligning 3D Generators with Simulation Feedback for Physical Soundness
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.22677v1)
- **Authors**: Ruining Li, Chuanxia Zheng, Christian Rupprecht, Andrea Vedaldi
- **Abstract**: Most 3D object generators focus on aesthetic quality, often neglecting physical constraints necessary in applications. One such constraint is that the 3D object should be self-supporting, i.e., remains balanced under gravity. Prior approaches to generating stable 3D objects used differentiable physics simulators to optimize geometry at test-time, which is slow, unstable, and prone to local optima. Inspired by the literature on aligning generative models to external feedback, we propose Direct Simulation Optimization (DSO), a framework to use the feedback from a (non-differentiable) simulator to increase the likelihood that the 3D generator outputs stable 3D objects directly. We construct a dataset of 3D objects labeled with a stability score obtained from the physics simulator. We can then fine-tune the 3D generator using the stability score as the alignment metric, via direct preference optimization (DPO) or direct reward optimization (DRO), a novel objective, which we introduce, to align diffusion models without requiring pairwise preferences. Our experiments show that the fine-tuned feed-forward generator, using either DPO or DRO objective, is much faster and more likely to produce stable objects than test-time optimization. Notably, the DSO framework works even without any ground-truth 3D objects for training, allowing the 3D generator to self-improve by automatically collecting simulation feedback on its own outputs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "DSO: Aligning 3D Generators with Simulation Feedback for Physical Soundness":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of generating 3D objects from images that are not only aesthetically pleasing but also physically sound, specifically focusing on stability under gravity.  The authors propose a framework called Direct Simulation Optimization (DSO). DSO uses a physics simulator to provide feedback on the stability of generated 3D models. This feedback is then used to fine-tune the 3D generator, increasing the likelihood of producing stable objects.  They introduce a novel objective, Direct Reward Optimization (DRO), for aligning diffusion models with external preferences. The method also demonstrates a self-improving pipeline where the generator is trained on its own outputs assessed by the physics simulator. The paper demonstrates that the fine-tuned generator produces stable objects faster and more reliably than test-time optimization methods or without any ground-truth 3D objects.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   The core idea of using simulation feedback to improve the physical soundness of a 3D generator is a valuable contribution. While physics-based losses and test-time optimization have been explored, DSO offers a feed-forward approach.  The concept of a self-improving pipeline using its own generated data and simulation feedback is another novel aspect.
    *   The introduction of Direct Reward Optimization (DRO) is an interesting alternative to Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), especially as it does not require pairwise preferences.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper addresses a critical limitation of current image-to-3D generators: the lack of physical soundness. This is relevant for various applications, including fabrication, simulation, and robotics.
    *   The significant performance gain in terms of stability and speed compared to test-time optimization makes DSO practically significant.
    *   The method is applicable even without ground-truth 3D objects for training, greatly expanding its applicability.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition and well-motivated approach.
    *   DSO framework is a straightforward and effective method
    *   The DRO objective provides a unique contribution.
    *   Demonstrated self-improving pipeline

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Reliance on a simulator: The method's effectiveness depends on the accuracy and efficiency of the physics simulator. While the paper uses MuJoCo, it may not perfectly model all real-world scenarios.
    *   The training data relies on potentially imperfect 3D datasets from Objaverse
    *   The paper is focused on stability under gravity as the sole physical attribute, and it could be explored for other physical constraints or properties.

*   **Potential Impact:**

    *   The paper provides an efficient and effective method for improving the physical soundness of generated 3D objects.
    *   The idea of using simulation feedback for training generative models is a promising direction for future research.
    *   The paper provides new insights into the problem of aligning generative models with external preferences.

*   **Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of 3D generation, effectively addressing the issue of physical soundness in 3D models and providing a practical solution. DSO introduces a self-improving pipeline with a unique perspective. Considering the paper's novelty, potential significance, the clear and effective methodology, and the experimental evaluation that demonstrates performance improvements over existing approaches, while considering the weakness of relying on an external simulator and imperfect 3D datasets for training, I assign a score of **8**.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Self-Evolving Multi-Agent Simulations for Realistic Clinical Interactions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.22678v1)
- **Authors**: Mohammad Almansoori, Komal Kumar, Hisham Cholakkal
- **Abstract**: In this work, we introduce MedAgentSim, an open-source simulated clinical environment with doctor, patient, and measurement agents designed to evaluate and enhance LLM performance in dynamic diagnostic settings. Unlike prior approaches, our framework requires doctor agents to actively engage with patients through multi-turn conversations, requesting relevant medical examinations (e.g., temperature, blood pressure, ECG) and imaging results (e.g., MRI, X-ray) from a measurement agent to mimic the real-world diagnostic process. Additionally, we incorporate self improvement mechanisms that allow models to iteratively refine their diagnostic strategies. We enhance LLM performance in our simulated setting by integrating multi-agent discussions, chain-of-thought reasoning, and experience-based knowledge retrieval, facilitating progressive learning as doctor agents interact with more patients. We also introduce an evaluation benchmark for assessing the LLM's ability to engage in dynamic, context-aware diagnostic interactions. While MedAgentSim is fully automated, it also supports a user-controlled mode, enabling human interaction with either the doctor or patient agent. Comprehensive evaluations in various simulated diagnostic scenarios demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach. Our code, simulation tool, and benchmark are available at \href{https://medagentsim.netlify.app/}.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MedAgentSim, an open-source simulation environment designed to evaluate and enhance the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) in dynamic diagnostic clinical settings.  MedAgentSim simulates a hospital environment with doctor, patient, and measurement agents. A key feature is that the "doctor" agent must actively engage with "patients" through multi-turn conversations to gather information and request specific medical examinations, mimicking real-world diagnostic processes.  The system also incorporates self-improvement mechanisms like multi-agent discussions, chain-of-thought reasoning, and experience-based knowledge retrieval. An evaluation benchmark is included to assess an LLM's ability to handle dynamic, context-aware diagnostic interactions. The system supports both automated and user-controlled modes.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Addresses a gap:**  The paper tackles a significant limitation of current LLM evaluations in the medical domain, which often rely on static datasets and pre-compiled patient information. MedAgentSim moves towards a more realistic, interactive scenario.
    * **Open-source and Reproducible:** The open-source nature of the simulation environment, along with the provided code and benchmark, promotes reproducibility and further research, which is commendable. This addresses a common issue of closed-source implementations in AI research.
    * **Multi-Agent and Self-Improvement:**  The use of a multi-agent framework and the incorporation of self-improvement mechanisms (memory, COT, ensembling) are valuable additions, allowing for more nuanced and progressive learning for the doctor agent. The ablation study provides evidence for the effectiveness of these mechanisms.
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper presents a thorough experimental setup, evaluating the performance of the system across different datasets and LLMs (both open-source and proprietary). The bias analysis is a thoughtful inclusion.
    * **Realistic Interaction:** The emphasis on the doctor agent actively gathering information through dialogue, rather than being provided with pre-compiled reports, represents a significant step towards more realistic clinical interaction simulation.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Novelty in Core Technologies:**  While the combination of elements is innovative, the individual technologies used (LLMs, multi-agent systems, chain-of-thought, KNN retrieval) are not groundbreaking in themselves.  The novelty primarily lies in the specific application and integration within the medical domain.
    * **Simplifications of Real-World Complexity:**  Despite the advancements, MedAgentSim is still a simplification of the complexities of real-world clinical interactions.  Factors like emotional intelligence, non-verbal communication, complex medical histories, and the uncertainty inherent in medical diagnosis are not fully captured.
    * **Reliance on LLM Evaluator for Accuracy:** The evaluation uses an LLM to assess the accuracy of the diagnoses. This introduces a potential bias and limits the objectivity of the results. A more rigorous evaluation methodology, possibly involving human expert review, would strengthen the findings.
    * **Limited Discussion of Ethical Implications:** While the paper mentions ethical considerations, the discussion remains high-level. A more in-depth analysis of potential biases, the risks of relying on AI for medical decision-making, and the importance of human oversight would be beneficial.

* **Significance:**
    * MedAgentSim has the potential to be a valuable tool for developing and evaluating LLMs for medical applications, especially in the area of diagnostic reasoning.
    * It provides a platform for researchers to explore different architectures, self-improvement strategies, and mitigation techniques for biases in LLMs.
    * The open-source nature of the project encourages community involvement and the development of more advanced simulation environments.
    * By highlighting the limitations of static evaluations, the paper encourages a shift towards more realistic and interactive assessment methodologies.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by providing a practical, open-source tool for evaluating LLMs in simulated clinical settings. While the core technologies are not entirely novel, the integration of these elements into a comprehensive framework and the focus on dynamic interaction are significant strengths. The weaknesses, such as simplifications of real-world complexities and reliance on an LLM evaluator, temper the overall impact. The significance lies in the ability to push the field beyond traditional static evaluations and facilitate the development of more robust and reliable LLMs for medical applications.

### Q-Insight: Understanding Image Quality via Visual Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.22679v1)
- **Authors**: Weiqi Li, Xuanyu Zhang, Shijie Zhao, Yabin Zhang, Junlin Li, Li Zhang, Jian Zhang
- **Abstract**: Image quality assessment (IQA) focuses on the perceptual visual quality of images, playing a crucial role in downstream tasks such as image reconstruction, compression, and generation. The rapid advancement of multi-modal large language models (MLLMs) has significantly broadened the scope of IQA, moving toward comprehensive image quality understanding that incorporates content analysis, degradation perception, and comparison reasoning beyond mere numerical scoring. Previous MLLM-based methods typically either generate numerical scores lacking interpretability or heavily rely on supervised fine-tuning (SFT) using large-scale annotated datasets to provide descriptive assessments, limiting their flexibility and applicability. In this paper, we propose Q-Insight, a reinforcement learning-based model built upon group relative policy optimization (GRPO), which demonstrates strong visual reasoning capability for image quality understanding while requiring only a limited amount of rating scores and degradation labels. By jointly optimizing score regression and degradation perception tasks with carefully designed reward functions, our approach effectively exploits their mutual benefits for enhanced performance. Extensive experiments demonstrate that Q-Insight substantially outperforms existing state-of-the-art methods in both score regression and degradation perception tasks, while exhibiting impressive zero-shot generalization to comparison reasoning tasks. Code will be available at https://github.com/lwq20020127/Q-Insight.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Q-Insight: Understanding Image Quality via Visual Reinforcement Learning":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Q-Insight, a novel reinforcement learning (RL) based model for image quality assessment (IQA). Unlike existing methods that heavily rely on large-scale labeled datasets or purely numerical scoring, Q-Insight is trained using group relative policy optimization (GRPO) with limited MOS (Mean Opinion Scores) and degradation labels.  The model is jointly trained on score regression and degradation perception tasks, with carefully designed reward functions to leverage their mutual benefits. Q-Insight demonstrates strong performance on OOD (out-of-distribution) datasets and exhibits impressive zero-shot generalization to tasks like image comparison.  The authors claim that Q-Insight combines numerical accuracy with interpretative reasoning, significantly improving the capabilities of IQA models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the application of GRPO to image quality understanding. While GRPO has been explored in other vision-language tasks, applying it to IQA and jointly optimizing score regression and degradation perception using specific reward functions is a valuable contribution. The ability to achieve competitive results with significantly less labeled data than supervised fine-tuning approaches is a definite advantage. Using the rule-based reward system for reinforcement learning is an interesting approach for tasks that are otherwise manually determined, or costly to collect, and it is well-designed in this scenario.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a key limitation of existing MLLM-based IQA methods: their dependence on massive annotated datasets or the lack of interpretability. Q-Insight offers a more data-efficient and interpretable alternative. Demonstrating strong OOD generalization is also significant, as it suggests that the model learns more robust and transferable features. The ability to perform zero-shot image comparison is another important indicator that the model isn't just learning to regurgitate training data but is actually learning to reason about image quality. However, other model-based image quality assessment methods, which do not utilize MLLMs are not considered.

* **Strengths:**
    *   **Data Efficiency:** The RL approach reduces the reliance on large, expensive labeled datasets.
    *   **Interpretability:** Q-Insight provides reasoning steps along with numerical scores, enhancing interpretability.
    *   **Generalization:** The model exhibits strong OOD generalization capabilities.
    *   **Multi-Task Learning:** The joint training approach leads to performance improvements in both score regression and degradation perception.
    *   **Practical Applications:** By providing both scores and explanations, the model has high applicability to downstream tasks.
* **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on Rule-Based Rewards:** The design of reward functions is crucial for the success of GRPO. While the paper proposes well-designed reward functions, they may not be optimal for all IQA scenarios and requires additional hyperparameter tuning.
    *   **Comparison with SOTA:** While the comparison with existing MLLM-based IQA methods is thorough, there are minimal considerations for other methods which may be more appropriate than the current benchmarks, especially non-MLLM techniques.
    *   **Complexity of RL:** RL-based training can be more complex and unstable than supervised learning, requiring careful tuning of hyperparameters.

* **Impact:** If the claims hold true, Q-Insight has the potential to significantly impact the field of IQA by offering a more data-efficient, interpretable, and generalizable solution. It could be used to automatically assess the quality of images in various applications, such as image compression, reconstruction, and generation, reducing the need for manual evaluation. Also, the rule-based reward can be implemented for other tasks, in order to reduce human labor.

* **Concerns** It is also worth noting the date on this preprint, which is written far into the future. Given this factor, it may be difficult to replicate the results described.

**Justification for Score:**

I'm assigning a score of **7.5**.  The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of IQA by introducing a novel RL-based framework that addresses key limitations of existing methods. The use of GRPO, the joint training approach, and the demonstrated OOD generalization are significant strengths. While the reliance on rule-based rewards and the increased complexity of RL training are potential weaknesses, the overall impact and novelty of the approach justify a high score. The weaknesses of comparing results from the future also heavily weigh into the low score.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10
